Never edit an acceptance test to make it pass — if an\nacceptance test is wrong, stop and say so; that is a failed round.\n\nUnder `parallel-change`, all of this happens in the NEW module: leave the\nold code alone and working until the acceptance tests are green, and make\nthe caller switch its own round. A test can drive `processMessage` with a provider that throws a string or a plain object and observe that `onError` is bypassed today because of the `error instanceof Error` gate in the `catch` block.\n+\n+## Done when these pass\n+\n+- [ ] **calls onError with an Error carrying the thrown string and runs afterSession while rethrowing** — when a turn rejects with a string (`'string boom'`), `onError` is called once with an `Error` instance whose `message` is `'string boom'`, `afterSession` runs after `onError`, and the rejection still propagates to the caller. Fails today because `onError` is skipped (`callOrder` is `['afterSession']` and `isError` is `false`).\n+      `src/orchestration/onErrorNonError.acceptance.test.ts`\n+\n+- [ ] **calls onError with an Error carrying a readable message when the stream rejects with a plain object** — when a turn rejects with a plain object (`{code: 503, detail: 'upstream down'}`), `onError` is called once with an `Error` instance carrying a non-empty `message`, `afterSession` runs after `onError`, and the rejection still propagates to the caller. Fails today because `onError` is skipped (`callOrder` is `['afterSession']` and `isError` is `false`).\n+      `src/orchestration/onErrorNonError.acceptance.test.ts`\n+\n+## Facets\n+\n+- [ ] In `src/orchestration/chatOrchestrator.ts`, coerce a caught non-`Error` rejection to an `Error` with a readable `message` (while passing an `Error` instance through unchanged) so `hooks.onError` always fires on any rejection inside the `try` block.\n+- [ ] Confirm existing `Error`-instance tests in `src/orchestration/chatOrchestrator.test.ts` (`called with session and error when stream fails`, `error still propagates after onError hook`, `error path calls onError then afterSession`) remain green without modification.\n+- [ ] Add a `CHANGELOG.md` entry under `## [Unreleased]` describing the behaviour change for existing consumers.\n+\n+## Out of scope\n+\n+- `beforeSession` escaping the `try` block (resolved separately in HALEngine#94).\n+- Usage accounting across tool rounds (resolved separately in HALEngine#93).\n+- Widening the `onError` hook parameter type away from `Error` (the ticket keeps `(session: ChatSession, error: Error) => Promise<void>`).\ndiff --git a/specs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md b/specs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md\nindex 66ff9d3..7b773cf 100644\n--- a/specs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md\n+++ b/specs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md\n@@ -16,13 +16,8 @@\n - For a session that completes without error the hooks fire in the order `beforeSession` → `beforeUserInput` → `afterUserInput` → `beforeModelResponse` → `afterModelResponse` → `afterSession` ([validated by: fires the lifecycle hooks in documented order for a session that completes without error](../../src/config.test.ts#L153)).\n - A `beforeSession` that throws reaches the caller as a rejection, and also runs `onError` with the thrown error and then `afterSession`, because the call sits inside the same `try`/`finally` as the rest of the turn. An `afterSession` hook called under this condition can infer the session never advanced past `beforeSession`: no user-input hooks ran, no model response was generated, and no tool rounds were started — a cleanup hook that allocates in `beforeSession` and frees in `afterSession` will therefore always see the allocation and the release on the same error path ([validated by: runs onError then afterSession when it throws, and still rejects to the caller](../../src/orchestration/chatOrchestrator.test.ts#L116)).\n - `afterModelResponse` is called once per user message after the round loop exits and is passed `totalUsage` — the sum of the usage every provider call in the turn reported, not the last round alone ([validated by: sums the usage of every round, not only the last](../../src/orchestration/chatOrchestrator.test.ts#L555)).\n+- Any rejection inside the turn calls `onError` with an `Error` — passed through when the thrown value is already an `Error`, or wrapped with a readable `message` when a string or object is thrown — and then runs `afterSession` while still propagating the rejection to the caller ([validated by: calls onError with an Error carrying the thrown string and runs afterSession while rethrowing](../../src/orchestration/onErrorNonError.acceptance.test.ts#L52), [validated by: calls onError with an Error carrying a readable message when the stream rejects with a plain object](../../src/orchestration/onErrorNonError.acceptance.test.ts#L63)).\n \n ## Engine startup\n \n - The engine's `start()` method rejects the returned promise when it cannot bind the configured port, rather than emitting an unhandled error event that would take the process down ([validated by: rejects instead of taking the process down with an unhandled error event](../../src/config.test.ts#L195)).\n-\n-## Measured semantics — out of scope\n-\n-This property of `src/orchestration/chatOrchestrator.ts` is recorded so the hook features that follow specify against real behaviour. This issue does not change it; that is a separate, out-of-scope change owned by no issue in this epic.\n-\n-- The `catch` gates `onError` on `error instanceof Error` ([chatOrchestrator.ts#L150](../../src/orchestration/chatOrchestrator.ts#L150)), so a rejection that is not an `Error` — a thrown string or object — bypasses `onError` entirely while still propagating to the caller.\ndiff --git a/src/orchestration/onErrorNonError.acceptance.test.ts b/src/orchestration/onErrorNonError.acceptance.test.ts\nnew file mode 100644\nindex 0000000..eb1663f\n--- /dev/null\n+++ b/src/orchestration/onErrorNonError.acceptance.test.ts\n@@ -0,0 +1,81 @@\n+import {createChatOrchestrator} from './chatOrchestrator.js';\n+import type {OrchestratorHooks} from './chatOrchestrator.js';\n+import type {AIProvider, MessageChunk} from '../types/ai.js';\n+import type {ChatSession} from '../types/session.js';\n+import {PromptBuilder} from '../infrastructure/builders/promptBuilder.js';\n+\n+const promptBuilder = new PromptBuilder({identity: 'You are a test assistant.'});\n+\n+const sessionWith = (content: string): ChatSession => ({\n+  sessionId: 'test-session',\n+  userId: 'user-1',\n+  entries: [{role: 'user', content, timestamp: new Date().toISOString()}],\n+});\n+\n+const rejectingWith = (reason: unknown): AIProvider => ({\n+  // eslint-disable-next-line require-yield\n+  async *sendMessage(): AsyncGenerator<MessageChunk> {\n+    throw reason;\n+  },\n+  async generateStructured<T>(): Promise<T> {\n+    return {} as T;\n+  },\n+});\n+\n+const turnOutcome = async (reason: unknown) => {\n+  const callOrder: string[] = [];\n+  let seenError: Error | undefined;\n+  const hooks: OrchestratorHooks = {\n+    onError: async (_session, error) => {\n+      callOrder.push('onError');\n+      seenError = error;\n+    },\n+    afterSession: async () => {\n+      callOrder.push('afterSession');\n+    },\n+  };\n+\n+  const orchestrator = createChatOrchestrator(rejectingWith(reason), promptBuilder, undefined, {hooks});\n+  const outcome = await orchestrator.processMessage(sessionWith('hello')).then(\n+    value => ({rejected: false, reason: value as unknown}),\n+    (thrown: unknown) => ({rejected: true, reason: thrown})\n+  );\n+\n+  return {\n+    outcome,\n+    callOrder,\n+    isError: seenError instanceof Error,\n+    message: seenError?.message,\n+  };\n+};\n+\n+describe('ChatOrchestrator onError on non-Error rejections', () => {\n+  it('calls onError with an Error carrying the thrown string and runs afterSession while rethrowing', async () => {\n+    const result = await turnOutcome('string boom');\n+\n+    expect(result).toEqual({\n+      outcome: {rejected: true, reason: 'string boom'},\n+      callOrder: ['onError', 'afterSession'],\n+      isError: true,\n+      message: 'string boom',\n+    });\n+  });\n+\n+  it('calls onError with an Error carrying a readable message when the stream rejects with a plain object', async () => {\n+    const reason = {code: 503, detail: 'upstream down'};\n+\n+    const result = await turnOutcome(reason);\n+\n+    expect({\n+      outcome: result.outcome,\n+      callOrder: result.callOrder,\n+      isError: result.isError,\n+      hasMessage: typeof result.message === 'string' && result.message.length > 0,\n+    }).toEqual({\n+      outcome: {rejected: true, reason},\n+      callOrder: ['onError', 'afterSession'],\n+      isError: true,\n+      hasMessage: true,\n+    });\n+  });\n+});"}

{"type":"tool_result","timestamp":"2026-11-11T08:40:19.741Z","tool_id":"run_shell_command__call_15892565829158485103","status":"success","output":"#!/bin/sh\n# Claude Code PreToolUse guard for the Bash tool, shipped to every agent pod\n# inside the lore-context skill and wired from the Claude hook bundle\n# (agent-skills/hooks/claude/.claude/settings.json, which the registry also\n# serves as the flat /skills/settings.json).\n#\n# Reads the hook event on stdin and refuses the test-runner invocations the\n# recipe forbids. Exit 2 blocks the call; stderr reaches the agent as the\n# reason. Everything else exits 0 and the call proceeds.\n#\n# LORE_TEST_POLICY, set per recipe on the Agent CR:\n#   scoped  (default) a runner may run only when its own command segment names\n#                     test files or a path, a workspace package or a Go package\n#                     path, or follows a cd into a subdirectory. Bare suite\n#                     runs are refused.\n#   none              no runner, no dependency install, no build, and no\n#                     linter/formatter/typechecker: CI publishes that verdict.\n#   any               guard off.\n#\n# LORE_ACCEPTANCE_DOD, set on the implementation loop's rounds: the path of the\n# branch's .lore/dod.md. Its \"Done when these pass\" tests are CI's to run, so a\n# runner naming one is refused, and so is one naming no test file at all, which\n# would sweep them in. Unset, or the file not written yet, changes nothing.\n#\n# The command is judged one segment at a time (split on ; & | and newlines,\n# parentheses and braces) so a test-file name in a comment or in a neighbouring\n# command cannot vouch for a bare runner, and quotes count as whitespace so\n# `sh -c \"npm test\"` reads as `npm test`.\n#\n# POSIX sh + grep -E / sed -E only: the hook bundle also runs on other vendors' images,\n# which promise neither bash nor jq.\n\npolicy=\"${LORE_TEST_POLICY:-scoped}\"\n[ \"$policy\" = \"any\" ] && exit 0\n# Segments and tokens are split by hand below; nothing here may glob.\nset -f\n\nevent=$(cat)\n\njson_string() {\n  printf '%s' \"$event\" | grep -oE \"\\\"$1\\\"[[:space:]]*:[[:space:]]*\\\"([^\\\"\\\\\\\\]|\\\\\\\\.)*\\\"\" | head -n 1 | sed -E \"s/^\\\"$1\\\"[[:space:]]*:[[:space:]]*\\\"//; s/\\\"\\$//\"\n}\n\n# JSON escapes: an escaped backslash collapses first so it cannot masquerade as\n# another escape, whitespace escapes and escaped quotes become spaces, and so do\n# shell quotes, so a quoted or wrapped runner reads like a bare one.\ncommand=$(json_string command | sed 's/\\\\\\\\/ /g; s/\\\\[ntr]/ /g; s/\\\\\"/ /g; s/\\\\\\//\\//g' | tr \"\\\"'\\`\" '   ')\n[ -z \"$command\" ] && exit 0\ncwd=$(json_string cwd | sed 's/\\\\\\//\\//g; s:/*$::')\n\nW='[[:space:]]'\nNW='[^[:space:]]'\nRUNNER=\"(^|$W)(((npx|pnpm|yarn|bunx?)$W+((exec|dlx)$W+)?)|python3?$W+-m$W+|(\\.\\.?/)?($NW*/)?node_modules/\\.bin/)?(vitest|jest|mocha|ava|tap|pytest)($W|$)\"\nRUNNER=\"$RUNNER|(^|$W)node$W+($NW*/)?(vitest|jest)(\\.m?js)?($W|$)|(^|$W)node$W+--test(=|$W|$)\"\nRUNNER=\"$RUNNER|(^|$W)(cargo$W+test|go$W+test|deno$W+test|playwright$W+test|cypress$W+run|make$W+(test|check|coverage)$NW*|turbo$W+(run$W+)?test|lerna$W+run$W+test|nx$W+(test|run-many))($W|$)\"\nSCRIPT=\"(^|$W)(npm|pnpm|yarn|bun)$W+((run|run-script)$W+)?(test|tst|t|coverage)(:$NW*)?($W|$)\"\nINSTALL=\"^((npm|pnpm|bun)$W+(ci|install|i|add)|yarn($W+(install|add))?|pip3?$W+install|uv$W+(sync|pip$W+install)|poetry$W+install|go$W+mod$W+download)($W|$)\"\nBUILD=\"^((npm|pnpm|yarn|bun)$W+(run$W+)?build(:$NW*)?|(npx$W+|(\\.\\.?/)?($NW*/)?node_modules/\\.bin/)?tsc|go$W+(build|install|generate)|cargo$W+(build|check)|make|docker$W+build)($W|$)\"\n# Linters and formatters: CI publishes their verdict, and reaching one through npx\n# downloads it into the pod, which is how a review pod fetched eslint@10 (#2155).\nLINT=\"^((npx$W+|(pnpm|bun)$W+dlx$W+|(\\.\\.?/)?($NW*/)?node_modules/\\.bin/)?(eslint|prettier|biome|ruff|black|golangci-lint)|(npm|pnpm|yarn|bun)$W+(run$W+)?(lint|format|fmt|typecheck|type-check|check)(:$NW*)?)($W|$)\"\nTEST_FILE=\"\\.(test|spec)\\.[cm]?[jt]sx?($W|:|$)|_test\\.go($W|$)|(^|$W|/)test_[A-Za-z0-9_]+\\.py\"\nWORKSPACE=\"(^|$W)(-w|--workspace|--filter|--project)(=|$W+)[^-[:space:]]\"\nGO_PACKAGE=\"go$W+test($W+-$NW+)*$W+\\./[A-Za-z0-9_][A-Za-z0-9_/.-]*($W|$)\"\nCARGO_PACKAGE=\"cargo$W+test($W+$NW+)*$W+(-p|--package)(=|$W)\"\nEXCLUDE=\"(^|$W)--exclude(=|$W|$)\"\n# Leading env assignments and the wrappers that run a command unchanged; what is\n# left is the command the segment actually runs.\n# A segment that only reads (grep, cat, git log ...) can mention a runner without running one.\nREAD_ONLY=\"^(grep|rg|egrep|fgrep|cat|sed|awk|ls|find|git|gh|head|tail|wc|diff|jq|yq|stat|tree|which|type|man)($W|$)\"\nHEAD=\"^([A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*=$NW*$W+)*((env|time|nohup|command|exec|sudo|eval|(sh|bash|dash|zsh)$W+-c)$W+|timeout$W+$NW+$W+)*\"\n\nmatches() {\n  printf '%s' \"$1\" | grep -qE \"$2\"\n}\n\nrefuse() {\n  printf '[lore] blocked: %s\\n' \"$1\" >&2\n  exit 2\n}\n\n# The command a segment runs, without its env assignments and wrappers.\nhead_of() {\n  printf '%s' \"$1\" | sed -E \"s/^$W+//; s/$HEAD//\"\n}\n\n# Where a `cd` lands: sub for a subdirectory (relative or absolute), root for the\n# repo root itself, the previous directory, home, or a target the hook cannot read.\ncd_scope() {\n  target=$(printf '%s' \"$1\" | sed -E \"s/^cd($W+|$)//; s/$W.*$//; s:/+$::\")\n  case \"$target\" in\n    ''|-|'~'|'$'*) echo root; return;;\n  esac\n  if matches \"$target\" '^(\\.\\.?/)*\\.?\\.?$' || [ \"$target\" = \"$cwd\" ]; then\n    echo root\n  else\n    echo sub\n  fi\n}\n\n# True when a positional argument after the runner names a file or a directory,\n# skipping the values of flags that take one (a config file is not a scope).\nnames_path() (\n  IFS=' '\n  seen=0\n  prev=''\n  for tok in $1; do\n    if [ \"$seen\" = 0 ]; then\n      case \"${tok##*/}\" in\n        vitest|vitest.mjs|jest|jest.js|mocha|ava|tap|pytest|npm|pnpm|yarn|bun|go) seen=1;;\n      esac\n      continue\n    fi\n    case \"$prev\" in\n      --config|-c|--root|-r|--dir|-t|--testNamePattern|--reporter|--outputFile|-o|--pool|--shard|--exclude)\n        prev=$tok\n        continue;;\n    esac\n    prev=$tok\n    case \"$tok\" in\n      -*|run|related|watch|test|tst|t|coverage|exec|dlx|run-script|...|*/...) ;;\n      */*|*.py) return 0;;\n    esac\n  done\n  return 1\n)\n\nscoped_runner() {\n  if matches \"$1\" \"$EXCLUDE\"; then\n    return 1\n  fi\n  matches \"$1\" \"$TEST_FILE\" || matches \"$1\" \"$WORKSPACE\" || matches \"$1\" \"$GO_PACKAGE\" \\\n    || matches \"$1\" \"$CARGO_PACKAGE\" || names_path \"$1\"\n}\n\n# Basenames of the test files under \"## Done when these pass\", dots escaped for grep -E.\n# Basenames, not paths: after a `cd` the runner names the file relative to the\n# package, so a full-path match would let the same file through.\nacceptance=''\nif [ -n \"$LORE_ACCEPTANCE_DOD\" ] && [ -r \"$LORE_ACCEPTANCE_DOD\" ]; then\n  acceptance=$(sed -n '/^## Done when these pass/,/^## /p' \"$LORE_ACCEPTANCE_DOD\" \\\n    | grep -oE '`[^`]+`' | tr -d '`' | grep -E \"$TEST_FILE\" \\\n    | sed -E 's:.*/::; s/\\./\\\\./g')\nfi\n\nacceptance_check() {\n  [ -z \"$acceptance\" ] && return 0\n  for name in $acceptance; do\n    if matches \"$1\" \"(^|$W|/)$name($W|:|$)\"; then\n      refuse \"acceptance tests are CI's to run, and the definition of done lists this one. Run the test you wrote this round and read CI's verdict on the acceptance tests with lore_get_ci_failures.\"\n    fi\n  done\n  if ! matches \"$1\" \"$TEST_FILE\"; then\n    refuse \"name the test file you wrote this round, e.g. \\`cd <package> && timeout 300 npx vitest run <file.test.ts>\\`. A run over a directory sweeps in the acceptance tests, which are CI's to run.\"\n  fi\n}\n\nsegments=$(printf '%s' \"$command\" | tr ';|&(){}' '\\n\\n\\n\\n\\n\\n\\n')\nscope=root\nIFS='\n'\nfor seg in $segments; do\n  seg=$(printf '%s' \"$seg\" | sed -E \"s/(^|$W)#.*$//\")\n  head=$(head_of \"$seg\")\n  [ -z \"$head\" ] && continue\n\n  if matches \"$head\" \"^cd($W|$)\"; then\n    scope=$(cd_scope \"$head\")\n    continue\n  fi\n  matches \"$head\" \"$READ_ONLY\" && continue\n\n  if [ \"$policy\" = \"none\" ]; then\n    if matches \"$seg\" \"$RUNNER\" || matches \"$seg\" \"$SCRIPT\"; then\n      refuse \"this node does not run tests. CI runs the suite; review from the source tree and the diff alone.\"\n    fi\n    if matches \"$head\" \"$INSTALL\" || matches \"$head\" \"$BUILD\"; then\n      refuse \"this node does not install dependencies or build. The pod has a 1Gi disk budget and CI already builds the branch.\"\n    fi\n    if matches \"$head\" \"$LINT\"; then\n      refuse \"lint, types and formatting are CI's verdict — read it with lore_get_ci_failures. Reaching a linter through npx also downloads it into a 1Gi pod.\"\n    fi\n    continue\n  fi\n\n  if matches \"$seg\" \"$RUNNER\" || matches \"$seg\" \"$SCRIPT\"; then\n    acceptance_check \"$seg\"\n    if [ \"$scope\" = \"sub\" ] || scoped_runner \"$seg\"; then\n      continue\n    fi\n    refuse \"the repository's full suite is not yours to run. CI runs it on every push. Name the test files or the directory you mean, e.g. \\`npx vitest run path/to/thing.test.ts\\`.\"\n  fi\ndone\n\nexit 0"}

A spec's UN-LINKED testable statements — the ones carrying no `([validated by ...])` parenthetical — are your work list: one acceptance test each. A ticket with no spec is defined by its own text instead.  THE CONTRACT, NON-NEGOTIABLE: - Write the smallest set of acceptance tests such that all of them passing   means the ticket is done and nothing more is owed. Prefer one; three is   a lot; more than five means the ticket needs splitting, not testing. - Put them in a NEW test file of their own, beside the code, never   appended to an existing test file. Its name MUST end in   `.acceptance.test.<ext>` — `repo-settings.acceptance.test.ts`,   `Plan.acceptance.test.tsx` — so a reader and the tooling can tell an   acceptance test from a round's own at a glance. Later rounds write their own tests and run   only those; the pod's Bash hook refuses the files listed under \"Done   when these pass\" by name, so an acceptance test sharing a file with   theirs would lock them out of their own test. - RUN them — those, and nothing else. Not the repository's full suite,   not lint, not the typechecker, not the formatter.   CI is the judge of this branch and runs all of it on every push.   Name the test files you run: the pod's Bash hook refuses a bare   `npm test` or a `vitest run` with no path.   Every acceptance test must fail, and fail for the RIGHT reason — the   behaviour is absent, not the import path typo'd or a fixture missing. - Quote the actual failure output in your final message. A test you did   not run is not a red bar, it is a guess — unless this pod cannot run   it at all, below. - Do not weaken, skip or `.todo` a test to make the suite tidy. Red is the   deliverable.  WHEN THIS POD CANNOT RUN THE TESTS. The pod carries Node and a few tools, not every language's toolchain. If the command that runs your tests is missing here — `java`, `mvn`, `gradle`, `go`, `cargo` or `dotnet` is not found, or a wrapper such as `./mvnw` reports no `JAVA_HOME` — do not look for it and do not install it: no `sudo`, no `apt`, no `find /`, no download. The pull request's CI runs these tests on every push and proves the red bar instead. Write the tests as the contract says, read each one against the ticket's claim in place of running it, then commit and push. In `.lore/dod.md`, right under the strategy, write that the red bar is CI's to show and name the CI job that runs the tests. In your final message, quote the command CI runs where the failure output would be.  WHAT AN ACCEPTANCE TEST IS. It observes behaviour through the code's real entry point — a function called, a component rendered, a binary run. A test whose assertion subject is the repository's own source text (readFileSync of a src/ or test file, a regex over another test, counting occurrences of a literal across files) is NOT an acceptance test and you must not write one; it pins prose, not behaviour, and it outlives the ticket as noise. The honest moves instead: - \"This prose drifts from a value\" — make the prose compute the value   (interpolate the count into the title, derive the label from the   fixture). Drift becomes impossible; no guard file exists. - \"This duplication must not come back\" — the diff removing it and the   existing tests staying green are the proof; a scan test is not. - If the only red bar you can build is a test about source text, the   ticket owes no new test at all — that is what `mechanical` is for.  SCOPE FIDELITY, EQUALLY NON-NEGOTIABLE. The ticket below carries the issue's title AND body — the reported problem is in there, not in your reading of it: - Open `.lore/dod.md` by QUOTING the ticket's central claim, verbatim.   The claim is what the reporter says is wrong, in their words. - Every acceptance test must fail BECAUSE of that claim. Run each red   test and read its failure output against the quote: failing for a   reason the ticket never states means you have redefined the ticket,   and a merged PR built on it closes the report without fixing it.   That is `changes_requested`, not a DoD. - A neighbouring problem you notice on the way is a finding for your   final message, never a substitute subject for the tests. - A bulk mechanical fix across hundreds of sites is \"really several   tickets\": park it via the `changes_requested` line below rather than   shrinking it into the slice you can express.  TRIAGE — pick exactly one strategy and say which: - `direct` — a seam already exists. The acceptance test can call the real   entry point today and fail on behaviour. Use this whenever it is honest. - `parallel-change` — no seam, but the change has a boundary. Build the   replacement BESIDE the existing code: point the acceptance tests at the   NEW module, leave the old code untouched and running while the new one   is red, and switch callers over only once it is green. The FIRST test   must go through the real caller path into the new module, even if that   module handles one case and throws on the rest — a replacement that is   not wired up drifts from the thing it replaces. - `characterize` — no seam and the change is diffuse. Do NOT write the   ticket's acceptance tests yet. First pin the CURRENT behaviour with   characterization tests that pass NOW, commit that green bar, and write   the ticket's acceptance tests against it. A refactor without a green bar   underneath it is not a refactor. The `legacy-characterize` skill is the   contract for this. - `mechanical` — the ticket is a small, fully-specified edit that owes no   new permanent test: a wrong label or count in prose, a doc correction,   a dedup whose behaviour EXISTING tests already pin. The DoD is the edit   itself plus those named existing tests staying green — name them under   Acceptance tests, RUN them, and quote that they PASS today. Write no   new test; the diff and the green bar are the proof, and the review   judges the edit. Do not stretch this onto a ticket that changes   behaviour: if no existing test pins what the edit touches, the ticket   is not mechanical.  Record the triage in `.lore/dod.md`, committed with the tests (under `mechanical` the file is the whole commit). Write it for the next pod, which has your branch and none of your reasoning — and for the human who opens the branch to see where the ticket stands. Use EXACTLY this shape. It is markdown and it is read rendered: the blank lines are what keep the claim, the strategy and the tests from collapsing into one paragraph, and the checkboxes are what make a round's progress legible at a glance. No emoji, no decoration; the structure is what makes it readable.   # Definition of Done    > <the ticket's central claim, quoted verbatim>    **Strategy: `direct`** — <one or two sentences: what seam exists or does   not. One line each. Add the inline   parenthetical on that statement in the repository's established form —   `Statement. ([validated by name](path/to/test.ts#L42))`. Inserting a   test shifts every #Lnn link below it: when the repository has   `scripts/spec-links/reanchor.mjs`, run   `node scripts/spec-links/reanchor.mjs` from the repository root after   the formatter — it moves each link to its test's current line, by test   title, and prints the links it could not map, which you fix by hand.   Without it, re-verify every existing #Lnn link on the statements you   touch yourself. - Stamp the test's spec anchor (the \"specs/<path>#<ordinal>\" it   validates) so the repo's tests.list surfaces it as the descriptor's   `spec` field and the VALIDATED_BY link is established automatically —   see `.lore/test-commands.yml` for how this repo exposes descriptors.  RULES: - Use your Read tool to read files before editing. Read the existing tests   first — mirror their framework, directory, file suffix and naming. - No mocks, no stubs. Exercise real values. A behaviour that seems to need   a double is telling you where the seam is missing; say so in   `.lore/dod.md` and pick `parallel-change` or `characterize` accordingly. - Write no production code. Not a stub, not a signature, not a TODO. - Do not edit `| Status |` on any spec. Nothing is implemented yet.  DELIVERY, NON-NEGOTIABLE — the next step runs in a DIFFERENT container: - Before you commit, run the repository's FORMATTER over the files you   changed (`npx prettier --write <files>`, `gofmt -w <files>`, whichever   this repo uses) and nothing else from CI's checklist. Do NOT run the   linter in this pod: CI runs it on every push, and here it does not   fit — in this repository a two-file `eslint` peaks near 800 MB against   a 1Gi pod, and runs 112256d9 and 62c1ca5e were OOM-killed on exactly   that. A lint finding comes back as a red build naming the file and   line, which the next round fixes with an editor. - Do NOT typecheck here either. A green test is not a compiled build —   vitest transpiles each file without checking types — but CI's build   step is what proves it, and in this repository `tsc --noEmit` on   `libs/shared` peaks near 950 MB against the 1Gi pod: run 2a291314's   dod pod was OOM-killed on it. A type error comes back as a red build   whose failed step and log tail name the file and line; the next   round fixes it there. Read your imports and signatures twice instead. - NEVER run a workspace build in this pod (`npm run build`, `tsc -b`, a   `tsc` without `--noEmit`): tests resolve every workspace package from   SOURCE, so nothing needs building for a test to see your change, and   the build is the 950 MB step that OOM-killed run abac6ee9 while it   rebuilt libs/shared for a test that never needed it. - When the work is done, `git -C /var/tmp/loop-bench/runs/a42024cf5e5a/workspace/target add` what you changed and commit it with a   short, factual message. Then `git -C /var/tmp/loop-bench/runs/a42024cf5e5a/workspace/target push origin HEAD`. The clone carries   its own credentials, so a plain push authenticates; you need no token   and must never look for one. - Then bring the branch up to date with its base — `git fetch origin main`   and merge `origin/main` in — and push again if that produced anything.   A branch that cannot merge cleanly gets NO CI AT ALL: GitHub runs no   workflow on a conflicted pull request, so the ticket parks on a build   that will never start. Resolve any conflict now, favouring `origin/main`   for anything this ticket did not write, and never by discarding your own   work. This is why a nine-day-old branch stalled: 186 commits of drift,   no checks, and a wait with nothing to wait for. - Confirm it landed: `git status` must report the branch is not ahead of   its upstream. An unpushed commit lives only in this container and   dies with it — do NOT report success for one. - If you genuinely changed nothing, say why and end your final message   with the line `LORE_NODE_RESULT: {\"outcome\":\"failed\"}` so the line does   not validate an empty branch — unless the ticket is already resolved,   which has its own line below. - Do not open a pull request: you have no `gh` and no GitHub token. If this pod cannot run your test — the command that runs it (`java`, `mvn`, `gradle`, `go`, `cargo`, `dotnet`) is not found, or a wrapper such as `./mvnw` reports no `JAVA_HOME` — do not look for it and do not install it: no `sudo`, no `apt`, no `find /`, no download. YOUR ROUND is the `tdd-loop` skill's three phases, in order, no steps skipped: one failing test for the smallest facet that moves an acceptance test closer to green, then the least code that passes it, then refactor with the bar green. Never edit an acceptance test to make it pass — if an acceptance test is wrong, stop and say so; that is a failed round.  Under `parallel-change`, all of this happens in the NEW module: leave the old code alone and working until the acceptance tests are green, and make the caller switch its own round. Under `characterize`, the characterization tests are part of the green bar you must keep — a refactor that reddens them is a behaviour change. Under `mechanical`, there is no red phase at all: perform the exact edits its Facets record and commit; the EXISTING tests `.lore/dod.md` names are CI's to run, and its verdict says whether they stayed green. The diff is the deliverable, and a round that invents a new test for it has left the strategy.  Never add a test whose assertion subject is the repo's own source text — readFileSync of a src/ or test file, a regex over another test, counting a literal across files. That pins prose, not behaviour: where prose must agree with a value, make the prose compute the value instead.  TRACEABILITY: - Each test validates a statement in a spec or ADR. Add the inline   parenthetical on that statement in the repository's established form —   `Statement. ([validated by name](path/to/test.ts#L42))`. Inserting a   test shifts every #Lnn link below it: when the repository has   `scripts/spec-links/reanchor.mjs`, run   `node scripts/spec-links/reanchor.mjs` from the repository root after   the formatter — it moves each link to its test's current line, by test   title, and prints the links it could not map, which you fix by hand.   Without it, re-verify every existing #Lnn link on the statements you   touch yourself. - Stamp the test's spec anchor (the \"specs/<path>#<ordinal>\" it   validates) so the repo's tests.list surfaces it as the descriptor's   `spec` field and the VALIDATED_BY link is established automatically —   see `.lore/test-commands.yml` for how this repo exposes descriptors.  - If this round completes what a `specs/<name>/spec.md` describes, update   that spec's `| Status |` header row in the same branch (Draft ->   Implemented/Shipped) so the status never lags the code.  BEFORE YOU EDIT A SYMBOL, ask the graph which tests already cover it: `lore-query-trace` with `tests_covering` set to the file you are about to change, and `ranges` as `start-end` when you know the span. THE CONTRACT, NON-NEGOTIABLE: - Write the smallest set of acceptance tests such that all of them passing   means the ticket is done and nothing more is owed. Its name MUST end in   `.acceptance.test.<ext>` — `repo-settings.acceptance.test.ts`,   `Plan.acceptance.test.tsx` — so a reader and the tooling can tell an   acceptance test from a round's own at a glance. Later rounds write their own tests and run   only those; the pod's Bash hook refuses the files listed under \"Done   when these pass\" by name, so an acceptance test sharing a file with   theirs would lock them out of their own test. - RUN them — those, and nothing else. Name the test files you run: the pod's Bash hook refuses a bare   `npm test` or a `vitest run` with no path.   Every acceptance test must fail, and fail for the RIGHT reason — the   behaviour is absent, not the import path typo'd or a fixture missing. - Quote the actual failure output in your final message. WHEN THIS POD CANNOT RUN THE TESTS. The pod carries Node and a few tools, not every language's toolchain. In `.lore/dod.md`, right under the strategy, write that the red bar is CI's to show and name the CI job that runs the tests. In your final message, quote the command CI runs where the failure output would be.  WHAT AN ACCEPTANCE TEST IS. It observes behaviour through the code's real entry point — a function called, a component rendered, a binary run. A test whose assertion subject is the repository's own source text (readFileSync of a src/ or test file, a regex over another test, counting occurrences of a literal across files) is NOT an acceptance test and you must not write one; it pins prose, not behaviour, and it outlives the ticket as noise. The honest moves instead: - \"This prose drifts from a value\" — make the prose compute the value   (interpolate the count into the title, derive the label from the   fixture). Drift becomes impossible; no guard file exists. - \"This duplication must not come back\" — the diff removing it and the   existing tests staying green are the proof; a scan test is not. - If the only red bar you can build is a test about source text, the   ticket owes no new test at all — that is what `mechanical` is for.  SCOPE FIDELITY, EQUALLY NON-NEGOTIABLE. The ticket below carries the issue's title AND body — the reported problem is in there, not in your reading of it: - Open `.lore/dod.md` by QUOTING the ticket's central claim, verbatim.   The claim is what the reporter says is wrong, in their words. - Every acceptance test must fail BECAUSE of that claim. Run each red   test and read its failure output against the quote: failing for a   reason the ticket never states means you have redefined the ticket,   and a merged PR built on it closes the report without fixing it.   That is `changes_requested`, not a DoD. - A neighbouring problem you notice on the way is a finding for your   final message, never a substitute subject for the tests. - A bulk mechanical fix across hundreds of sites is \"really several   tickets\": park it via the `changes_requested` line below rather than   shrinking it into the slice you can express.  TRIAGE — pick exactly one strategy and say which: - `direct` — a seam already exists. The acceptance test can call the real   entry point today and fail on behaviour. Use this whenever it is honest. - `parallel-change` — no seam, but the change has a boundary. Build the   replacement BESIDE the existing code: point the acceptance tests at the   NEW module, leave the old code untouched and running while the new one   is red, and switch callers over only once it is green. The FIRST test   must go through the real caller path into the new module, even if that   module handles one case and throws on the rest — a replacement that is   not wired up drifts from the thing it replaces. - `characterize` — no seam and the change is diffuse. Do NOT write the   ticket's acceptance tests yet. First pin the CURRENT behaviour with   characterization tests that pass NOW, commit that green bar, and write   the ticket's acceptance tests against it. A refactor without a green bar   underneath it is not a refactor. The `legacy-characterize` skill is the   contract for this. - `mechanical` — the ticket is a small, fully-specified edit that owes no   new permanent test: a wrong label or count in prose, a doc correction,   a dedup whose behaviour EXISTING tests already pin. The DoD is the edit   itself plus those named existing tests staying green — name them under   Acceptance tests, RUN them, and quote that they PASS today. Write no   new test; the diff and the green bar are the proof, and the review   judges the edit. Do not stretch this onto a ticket that changes   behaviour: if no existing test pins what the edit touches, the ticket   is not mechanical. Build the   replacement BESIDE the existing code: point the acceptance tests at the   NEW module, leave the old code untouched and running while the new one   is red, and switch callers over only once it is green. The FIRST test   must go through the real caller path into the new module, even if that   module handles one case and throws on the rest — a replacement that is   not wired up drifts from the thing it replaces. - `characterize` — no seam and the change is diffuse. Do NOT write the   ticket's acceptance tests yet. First pin the CURRENT behaviour with   characterization tests that pass NOW, commit that green bar, and write   the ticket's acceptance tests against it. A refactor without a green bar   underneath it is not a refactor. The `legacy-characterize` skill is the   contract for this. - `mechanical` — the ticket is a small, fully-specified edit that owes no   new permanent test: a wrong label or count in prose, a doc correction,   a dedup whose behaviour EXISTING tests already pin. The DoD is the edit   itself plus those named existing tests staying green — name them under   Acceptance tests, RUN them, and quote that they PASS today. Write no   new test; the diff and the green bar are the proof, and the review   judges the edit. Do not stretch this onto a ticket that changes   behaviour: if no existing test pins what the edit touches, the ticket   is not mechanical.  Record the triage in `.lore/dod.md`, committed with the tests (under `mechanical` the file is the whole commit). Write it for the next pod, which has your branch and none of your reasoning — and for the human who opens the branch to see where the ticket stands. Use EXACTLY this shape. Without it, re-verify every existing #Lnn link on the statements you   touch yourself. - Stamp the test's spec anchor (the \"specs/<path>#<ordinal>\" it   validates) so the repo's tests.list surfaces it as the descriptor's   `spec` field and the VALIDATED_BY link is established automatically —   see `.lore/test-commands.yml` for how this repo exposes descriptors.  RULES: - Use your Read tool to read files before editing. Read the existing tests   first — mirror their framework, directory, file suffix and naming. - No mocks, no stubs. Exercise real values. That is `changes_requested`, not a DoD. - A neighbouring problem you notice on the way is a finding for your   final message, never a substitute subject for the tests. - A bulk mechanical fix across hundreds of sites is \"really several   tickets\": park it via the `changes_requested` line below rather than   shrinking it into the slice you can express.  TRIAGE — pick exactly one strategy and say which: - `direct` — a seam already exists. The acceptance test can call the real   entry point today and fail on behaviour. Use this whenever it is honest. - `parallel-change` — no seam, but the change has a boundary. Build the   replacement BESIDE the existing code: point the acceptance tests at the   NEW module, leave the old code untouched and running while the new one   is red, and switch callers over only once it is green. The FIRST test   must go through the real caller path into the new module, even if that   module handles one case and throws on the rest — a replacement that is   not wired up drifts from the thing it replaces. - `characterize` — no seam and the change is diffuse. Do NOT write the   ticket's acceptance tests yet. First pin the CURRENT behaviour with   characterization tests that pass NOW, commit that green bar, and write   the ticket's acceptance tests against it. A refactor without a green bar   underneath it is not a refactor. The `legacy-characterize` skill is the   contract for this. - `mechanical` — the ticket is a small, fully-specified edit that owes no   new permanent test: a wrong label or count in prose, a doc correction,   a dedup whose behaviour EXISTING tests already pin. The DoD is the edit   itself plus those named existing tests staying green — name them under   Acceptance tests, RUN them, and quote that they PASS today. Write no   new test; the diff and the green bar are the proof, and the review   judges the edit. Do not stretch this onto a ticket that changes   behaviour: if no existing test pins what the edit touches, the ticket   is not mechanical.  Record the triage in `.lore/dod.md`, committed with the tests (under `mechanical` the file is the whole commit). Write it for the next pod, which has your branch and none of your reasoning — and for the human who opens the branch to see where the ticket stands. Use EXACTLY this shape. It is markdown and it is read rendered: the blank lines are what keep the claim, the strategy and the tests from collapsing into one paragraph, and the checkboxes are what make a round's progress legible at a glance. No emoji, no decoration; the structure is what makes it readable.   # Definition of Done    > <the ticket's central claim, quoted verbatim>    **Strategy: `direct`** — <one or two sentences: what seam exists or does   not. One of direct | parallel-change | characterize | mechanical.>    ## Done when these pass    - [ ] **<test name>** — <the behaviour it pins; under `mechanical`, the     EXISTING tests that must stay green>     `path/to/test.ts`    ## Facets    - [ ] <the red-green-refactor steps you expect, smallest first; under     `mechanical`, the exact edits owed. One line each. A round ticks the     box it closed rather than rewriting the list.>    ## Out of scope    - <what this ticket does NOT cover>  TRACEABILITY: - Each test validates a statement in a spec or ADR. Add the inline   parenthetical on that statement in the repository's established form —   `Statement. ([validated by name](path/to/test.ts#L42))`. Inserting a   test shifts every #Lnn link below it: when the repository has   `scripts/spec-links/reanchor.mjs`, run   `node scripts/spec-links/reanchor.mjs` from the repository root after   the formatter — it moves each link to its test's current line, by test   title, and prints the links it could not map, which you fix by hand.   Without it, re-verify every existing #Lnn link on the statements you   touch yourself. - Stamp the test's spec anchor (the \"specs/<path>#<ordinal>\" it   validates) so the repo's tests.list surfaces it as the descriptor's   `spec` field and the VALIDATED_BY link is established automatically —   see `.lore/test-commands.yml` for how this repo exposes descriptors.  RULES: - Use your Read tool to read files before editing. Read the existing tests   first — mirror their framework, directory, file suffix and naming. - No mocks, no stubs. Exercise real values. A behaviour that seems to need   a double is telling you where the seam is missing; say so in   `.lore/dod.md` and pick `parallel-change` or `characterize` accordingly. - Write no production code. Not a stub, not a signature, not a TODO. - Do not edit `| Status |` on any spec. Nothing is implemented yet.  DELIVERY, NON-NEGOTIABLE — the next step runs in a DIFFERENT container: - Before you commit, run the repository's FORMATTER over the files you   changed (`npx prettier --write <files>`, `gofmt -w <files>`, whichever   this repo uses) and nothing else from CI's checklist. Do NOT run the   linter in this pod: CI runs it on every push, and here it does not   fit — in this repository a two-file `eslint` peaks near 800 MB against   a 1Gi pod, and runs 112256d9 and 62c1ca5e were OOM-killed on exactly   that. A lint finding comes back as a red build naming the file and   line, which the next round fixes with an editor. - Do NOT typecheck here either. A green test is not a compiled build —   vitest transpiles each file without checking types — but CI's build   step is what proves it, and in this repository `tsc --noEmit` on   `libs/shared` peaks near 950 MB against the 1Gi pod: run 2a291314's   dod pod was OOM-killed on it. A type error comes back as a red build   whose failed step and log tail name the file and line; the next   round fixes it there. Read your imports and signatures twice instead. - NEVER run a workspace build in this pod (`npm run build`, `tsc -b`, a   `tsc` without `--noEmit`): tests resolve every workspace package from   SOURCE, so nothing needs building for a test to see your change, and   the build is the 950 MB step that OOM-killed run abac6ee9 while it   rebuilt libs/shared for a test that never needed it. - When the work is done, `git -C /var/tmp/loop-bench/runs/a42024cf5e5a/workspace/target add` what you changed and commit it with a   short, factual message. Then `git -C /var/tmp/loop-bench/runs/a42024cf5e5a/workspace/target push origin HEAD`. The clone carries   its own credentials, so a plain push authenticates; you need no token   and must never look for one. - Then bring the branch up to date with its base — `git fetch origin main`   and merge `origin/main` in — and push again if that produced anything.   A branch that cannot merge cleanly gets NO CI AT ALL: GitHub runs no   workflow on a conflicted pull request, so the ticket parks on a build   that will never start. Resolve any conflict now, favouring `origin/main`   for anything this ticket did not write, and never by discarding your own   work. This is why a nine-day-old branch stalled: 186 commits of drift,   no checks, and a wait with nothing to wait for. - Confirm it landed: `git status` must report the branch is not ahead of   its upstream. An unpushed commit lives only in this container and   dies with it — do NOT report success for one. - If you genuinely changed nothing, say why and end your final message   with the line `LORE_NODE_RESULT: {\"outcome\":\"failed\"}` so the line does   not validate an empty branch — unless the ticket is already resolved,   which has its own line below. - Do not open a pull request: you have no `gh` and no GitHub token. Lore   opens the PR from the branch you push.  THE LAST LINE YOU PRINT decides where this ticket goes. Print exactly one, on its own line, as the final thing in your message: - Red acceptance tests are committed and pushed — or, under `mechanical`,   `.lore/dod.md` naming green existing tests is (state the strategy you   picked in the extra):   LORE_NODE_RESULT: {\"outcome\":\"success\",\"extras\":{\"dod_strategy\":\"direct\"}} - The ticket cannot be expressed as acceptance tests that fail for ITS   stated reason — it is ambiguous, it is really several tickets (a bulk   mechanical fix included), it asks for something unobservable, or every   honest red test you can write fails for a reason the ticket never   states. Say precisely what you would need, then:   LORE_NODE_RESULT: {\"outcome\":\"changes_requested\",\"extras\":{\"dod_blocked\":\"<one line: what is missing>\"}}   This parks the ticket for a human. It is the right answer for a bad   ticket and the wrong answer for a hard one. The dod_blocked line is   posted verbatim on the issue as the request for a rewrite, so write it   for the ticket's author: what is missing, not what you tried. - The ticket's central claim is ALREADY TRUE on the branch's base: the   behaviour it asks for is present on `origin/main` (a merged sibling   pull request, a commit naming the issue), so no honest red test   exists and nothing is owed. Write no tests and commit nothing. Name   what resolved it — the commit or pull request — then:   LORE_NODE_RESULT: {\"outcome\":\"changes_requested\",\"extras\":{\"dod_resolved\":\"<one line: what already resolved it, naming the commit or PR>\"}}   This CLOSES the ticket and its draft pull request, with your line   posted as the reason, so write it for whoever reads the issue later. Later rounds write their own tests and run   only those; the pod's Bash hook refuses the files listed under \"Done   when these pass\" by name, so an acceptance test sharing a file with   theirs would lock them out of their own test. - RUN them — those, and nothing else. Name the test files you run: the pod's Bash hook refuses a bare   `npm test` or a `vitest run` with no path.   Every acceptance test must fail, and fail for the RIGHT reason — the   behaviour is absent, not the import path typo'd or a fixture missing. - Quote the actual failure output in your final message. WHEN THIS POD CANNOT RUN THE TESTS. The pod carries Node and a few tools, not every language's toolchain. In your final message, quote the command CI runs where the failure output would be.  WHAT AN ACCEPTANCE TEST IS. It observes behaviour through the code's real entry point — a function called, a component rendered, a binary run. A test whose assertion subject is the repository's own source text (readFileSync of a src/ or test file, a regex over another test, counting occurrences of a literal across files) is NOT an acceptance test and you must not write one; it pins prose, not behaviour, and it outlives the ticket as noise. The honest moves instead: - \"This prose drifts from a value\" — make the prose compute the value   (interpolate the count into the title, derive the label from the   fixture). Drift becomes impossible; no guard file exists. - \"This duplication must not come back\" — the diff removing it and the   existing tests staying green are the proof; a scan test is not. - If the only red bar you can build is a test about source text, the   ticket owes no new test at all — that is what `mechanical` is for.  SCOPE FIDELITY, EQUALLY NON-NEGOTIABLE. The ticket below carries the issue's title AND body — the reported problem is in there, not in your reading of it: - Open `.lore/dod.md` by QUOTING the ticket's central claim, verbatim.   The claim is what the reporter says is wrong, in their words. - Every acceptance test must fail BECAUSE of that claim. Run each red   test and read its failure output against the quote: failing for a   reason the ticket never states means you have redefined the ticket,   and a merged PR built on it closes the report without fixing it.   That is `changes_requested`, not a DoD. - A neighbouring problem you notice on the way is a finding for your   final message, never a substitute subject for the tests. - A bulk mechanical fix across hundreds of sites is \"really several   tickets\": park it via the `changes_requested` line below rather than   shrinking it into the slice you can express.  TRIAGE — pick exactly one strategy and say which: - `direct` — a seam already exists. The acceptance test can call the real   entry point today and fail on behaviour. Use this whenever it is honest. - `parallel-change` — no seam, but the change has a boundary. Build the   replacement BESIDE the existing code: point the acceptance tests at the   NEW module, leave the old code untouched and running while the new one   is red, and switch callers over only once it is green. The FIRST test   must go through the real caller path into the new module, even if that   module handles one case and throws on the rest — a replacement that is   not wired up drifts from the thing it replaces. - `characterize` — no seam and the change is diffuse. Do NOT write the   ticket's acceptance tests yet. First pin the CURRENT behaviour with   characterization tests that pass NOW, commit that green bar, and write   the ticket's acceptance tests against it. A refactor without a green bar   underneath it is not a refactor. The `legacy-characterize` skill is the   contract for this. - `mechanical` — the ticket is a small, fully-specified edit that owes no   new permanent test: a wrong label or count in prose, a doc correction,   a dedup whose behaviour EXISTING tests already pin. The DoD is the edit   itself plus those named existing tests staying green — name them under   Acceptance tests, RUN them, and quote that they PASS today. Write no   new test; the diff and the green bar are the proof, and the review   judges the edit. Do not stretch this onto a ticket that changes   behaviour: if no existing test pins what the edit touches, the ticket   is not mechanical. A ticket with no spec is defined by its own text instead.  THE CONTRACT, NON-NEGOTIABLE: - Write the smallest set of acceptance tests such that all of them passing   means the ticket is done and nothing more is owed. Prefer one; three is   a lot; more than five means the ticket needs splitting, not testing. - Put them in a NEW test file of their own, beside the code, never   appended to an existing test file. Its name MUST end in   `.acceptance.test.<ext>` — `repo-settings.acceptance.test.ts`,   `Plan.acceptance.test.tsx` — so a reader and the tooling can tell an   acceptance test from a round's own at a glance. Later rounds write their own tests and run   only those; the pod's Bash hook refuses the files listed under \"Done   when these pass\" by name, so an acceptance test sharing a file with   theirs would lock them out of their own test. - RUN them — those, and nothing else. Not the repository's full suite,   not lint, not the typechecker, not the formatter.   CI is the judge of this branch and runs all of it on every push.   Name the test files you run: the pod's Bash hook refuses a bare   `npm test` or a `vitest run` with no path.   Every acceptance test must fail, and fail for the RIGHT reason — the   behaviour is absent, not the import path typo'd or a fixture missing. - Quote the actual failure output in your final message. A test you did   not run is not a red bar, it is a guess — unless this pod cannot run   it at all, below. - Do not weaken, skip or `.todo` a test to make the suite tidy. Red is the   deliverable.  WHEN THIS POD CANNOT RUN THE TESTS. The pod carries Node and a few tools, not every language's toolchain. If the command that runs your tests is missing here — `java`, `mvn`, `gradle`, `go`, `cargo` or `dotnet` is not found, or a wrapper such as `./mvnw` reports no `JAVA_HOME` — do not look for it and do not install it: no `sudo`, no `apt`, no `find /`, no download. The pull request's CI runs these tests on every push and proves the red bar instead. Write the tests as the contract says, read each one against the ticket's claim in place of running it, then commit and push. In `.lore/dod.md`, right under the strategy, write that the red bar is CI's to show and name the CI job that runs the tests. In your final message, quote the command CI runs where the failure output would be.  WHAT AN ACCEPTANCE TEST IS. It observes behaviour through the code's real entry point — a function called, a component rendered, a binary run. A test whose assertion subject is the repository's own source text (readFileSync of a src/ or test file, a regex over another test, counting occurrences of a literal across files) is NOT an acceptance test and you must not write one; it pins prose, not behaviour, and it outlives the ticket as noise. The honest moves instead: - \"This prose drifts from a value\" — make the prose compute the value   (interpolate the count into the title, derive the label from the   fixture). Drift becomes impossible; no guard file exists. - \"This duplication must not come back\" — the diff removing it and the   existing tests staying green are the proof; a scan test is not. - If the only red bar you can build is a test about source text, the   ticket owes no new test at all — that is what `mechanical` is for.  SCOPE FIDELITY, EQUALLY NON-NEGOTIABLE. The ticket below carries the issue's title AND body — the reported problem is in there, not in your reading of it: - Open `.lore/dod.md` by QUOTING the ticket's central claim, verbatim.   The claim is what the reporter says is wrong, in their words. - Every acceptance test must fail BECAUSE of that claim. Run each red   test and read its failure output against the quote: failing for a   reason the ticket never states means you have redefined the ticket,   and a merged PR built on it closes the report without fixing it.   That is `changes_requested`, not a DoD. - A neighbouring problem you notice on the way is a finding for your   final message, never a substitute subject for the tests. - A bulk mechanical fix across hundreds of sites is \"really several   tickets\": park it via the `changes_requested` line below rather than   shrinking it into the slice you can express.  TRIAGE — pick exactly one strategy and say which: - `direct` — a seam already exists. The acceptance test can call the real   entry point today and fail on behaviour. Use this whenever it is honest. - `parallel-change` — no seam, but the change has a boundary. Build the   replacement BESIDE the existing code: point the acceptance tests at the   NEW module, leave the old code untouched and running while the new one   is red, and switch callers over only once it is green. The FIRST test   must go through the real caller path into the new module, even if that   module handles one case and throws on the rest — a replacement that is   not wired up drifts from the thing it replaces. - `characterize` — no seam and the change is diffuse. Do NOT write the   ticket's acceptance tests yet. First pin the CURRENT behaviour with   characterization tests that pass NOW, commit that green bar, and write   the ticket's acceptance tests against it. A refactor without a green bar   underneath it is not a refactor. The `legacy-characterize` skill is the   contract for this. - `mechanical` — the ticket is a small, fully-specified edit that owes no   new permanent test: a wrong label or count in prose, a doc correction,   a dedup whose behaviour EXISTING tests already pin. The DoD is the edit   itself plus those named existing tests staying green — name them under   Acceptance tests, RUN them, and quote that they PASS today. Write no   new test; the diff and the green bar are the proof, and the review   judges the edit. Do not stretch this onto a ticket that changes   behaviour: if no existing test pins what the edit touches, the ticket   is not mechanical.  Record the triage in `.lore/dod.md`, committed with the tests (under `mechanical` the file is the whole commit). Write it for the next pod, which has your branch and none of your reasoning — and for the human who opens the branch to see where the ticket stands. Use EXACTLY this shape. It is markdown and it is read rendered: the blank lines are what keep the claim, the strategy and the tests from collapsing into one paragraph, and the checkboxes are what make a round's progress legible at a glance. No emoji, no decoration; the structure is what makes it readable.   # Definition of Done    > <the ticket's central claim, quoted verbatim>    **Strategy: `direct`** — <one or two sentences: what seam exists or does   not. One of direct | parallel-change | characterize | mechanical.>    ## Done when these pass    - [ ] **<test name>** — <the behaviour it pins; under `mechanical`, the     EXISTING tests that must stay green>     `path/to/test.ts`    ## Facets    - [ ] <the red-green-refactor steps you expect, smallest first; under     `mechanical`, the exact edits owed. One line each. A round ticks the     box it closed rather than rewriting the list.>    ## Out of scope    - <what this ticket does NOT cover>  TRACEABILITY: - Each test validates a statement in a spec or ADR.
